Yeah, I do… more than I’d like to admit. I’ll hear two colleagues talking in the hallway, or a parent explaining something, and I’ll jump in because in my head I’m thinking, “Oh, I know how to help with this,” or “Let me clarify that.” But then I realize I’ve talked over them or kind of taken charge before they even finished their sentence.

It happens at home too—my spouse might be talking with a relative, and I’ll butt in like I’m solving the problem, when nobody asked me to. I don’t mean it as rude, but it comes out that way sometimes.

I’ll finish something at school, or at home, and before I even take a breath I’m onto the next thing.\n\nI’m a special education teacher, so there’s always something to do anyway, you know—papers, kids needing help, emails, and then I get home and it’s the same kind of thing, just different tasks. But it feels like it’s more than just being busy. It’s like my body and brain won’t let me stop moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. I don’t always realize it in the moment, but I’ll go into, say, a parent conference or a staff meeting thinking, “Okay, just keep it short,” and then suddenly I’m giving three examples, then I’m telling some story from my classroom, then I’m offering ideas nobody even asked for.\n\nAnd I can kind of see the other person doing that thing where they’re ready to wrap up—looking at the clock or shifting papers—but I’m still talking. It’s embarrassing afterward, because I really didn’t mean to take over the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do… more than I’d like to admit. I don’t mean it as rude, but it comes out that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself acting on impulse, like doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Um… acting on impulse. I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. And then I start thinking about how schools expect you to be flexible but also have everything planned, which is its own whole thing…\n\nSorry, I’m getting off track. I guess I’d need an example of what you mean by impulse. I’m not sure I’ve noticed that as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your personal life, outside of work or teaching?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not sure. I mean, probably everybody does that sometimes, right? Like buying something without really comparing prices, or saying yes to a dinner before checking the calendar—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by impulsive.\n\nAt home I tend to… well, actually, my wife would probably have a better answer to this than I do. She notices things before I do. Like I’ll start a house project because something is bothering me, and then suddenly the garage is a mess, and I’m looking for a tool I know I had in 1998—sorry, that’s not really answering.\n\nI guess I don’t clearly think of myself as “acting on impulse” in my personal life. leaves seat in situations when remaining seated is expected.\n  manifestation: When I’m supposed to sit through a training, dinner with the family, or a school meeting, I keep finding reasons to get up—refill water, check the copier, look in on my relative—even when I know it makes me look disrespectful.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Quiet time at home has become almost impossible for me, because if my spouse is trying to watch a show or my relative is resting, I’ll start narrating what I’m doing, humming, or moving things around until someone asks me to please settle down.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that I feel like I’m constantly running on a motor lately, like I finish one thing at school or home and immediately jump into the next without ever being able to shut myself off.\n- key: S015\n  name: Excessive_Talking\n  description: Often talks excessively.\n  manifestation: I’ll go into a parent conference or a staff meeting meaning to be brief, and then I hear myself giving three examples, a story from my classroom, and ideas nobody asked for while the other person is clearly trying to wrap it up.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: I keep jumping into conversations between colleagues, parents, or my spouse and our relative because I think I’m helping, but I end up talking over them or taking charge before they’ve even had a chance to finish.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: My mother used to joke that even in elementary school I taught the class from my seat because I was always out of my chair, talking, or cutting in before the teacher finished directions. This was part of who I was long before adulthood, not something that just started with this family change.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work, the complaints have gotten serious enough that I was put on a corrective plan, and at home my spouse says caregiving routines are harder because people have to manage my energy on top of everything else. I feel like I’m damaging trust with colleagues and family even though I’m trying to help.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 8 months\n  manifestation: This level of trouble has been going on for about 8 months, since our older relative moved in with us.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 58 / special_education_teacher / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S014\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself acting on impulse, like doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Um… acting on impulse. I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I make quick decisions sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it “without thinking.” \n\nLike, in teaching you’re constantly reacting—one kid needs help, another situation pops up, the schedule changes—so you sort of have to move fast. And then I start thinking about how schools expect you to be flexible but also have everything planned, which is its own whole thing…\n\nSorry, I’m getting off track. I guess I’d need an example of what you mean by impulse. I’m not sure I’ve noticed that as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your personal life, outside of work or teaching?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not sure. I mean, probably everybody does that sometimes, right? Like buying something without really comparing prices, or saying yes to a dinner before checking the calendar—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by impulsive.\n\nAt home I tend to… well, actually, my wife would probably have a better answer to this than I do. She notices things before I do. Like I’ll start a house project because something is bothering me, and then suddenly the garage is a mess, and I’m looking for a tool I know I had in 1998—sorry, that’s not really answering.\n\nI guess I don’t clearly think of myself as “acting on impulse” in my personal life. I’d need you to define it a little more, because I’m not sure what counts.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems in your personal relationships due to saying or doing something without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like I’m trying to be hurtful, but I’ll jump into conversations—like my wife talking with a relative, or at work with colleagues or parents—and in my head I’m thinking, “Oh, I can help,” or “I know what they mean,” and then I’m already talking before they’re done.\n\nAnd then it turns into me taking over the conversation, which… yeah, people don’t always appreciate that. My wife will give me that look, like, “Let them finish,” and I realize I kind of barged in. It causes tension because I think I’m helping, but it comes across like I’m not listening or I’m trying to run the whole thing.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often have trouble completing tasks or projects you've started?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m not sure how to answer that one. I mean… tasks and projects, that could mean a lot of things. At work there are always things that are “started” but not really finished because somebody changes the plan, or the meeting gets moved, or a student needs something right then.

At home, I guess I have projects that sit around, but I don’t know if that’s unusual. Like, there’s always something half-done in a house, right? You fix one thing and then notice the drawer sticks, and then you’re in the garage looking for screws, and then—well, anyway.

I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed it as a major problem, but I might need a more specific example of what you mean by “trouble completing.”

My wife will give me that look, like, “Let them finish,” and I realize I kind of barged in. It causes tension because I think I’m helping, but it comes across like I’m not listening or I’m trying to run the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks or projects you've started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one. You fix one thing and then notice the drawer sticks, and then you’re in the garage looking for screws, and then—well, anyway.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed it as a major problem, but I might need a more specific example of what you mean by “trouble completing.”\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Easily distracted by things around me… I’m not sure. I mean, it depends where I am, I guess. If there’s a lot going on, like noise in the hallway or someone moving around, I notice it, but I don’t know if that’s more than other people.

I guess I don’t have a clear answer. I notice things around me, but I don’t know if I’d call it a big problem unless you mean something specific.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t always notice it at first, which is the weird part. Like in IEP meetings, or those admin classes I have to sit through, I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing under the table the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen over and over.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Waiting my turn… I don’t know. In a line, I mean, I don’t love lines, but who does? If I’m at the grocery store and someone’s using coupons or there’s a price check, I’ll notice myself getting kind of impatient, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or just being a person with frozen food in the cart.

In conversations… maybe sometimes I jump in if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure that’s the same as “trouble waiting.” With teachers we’re always sort of overlapping—somebody mentions a student, then somebody else remembers a meeting, then the bell rings, and it’s all a little chaotic anyway.

So… I guess I’m not really sure. I haven’t thought of it as a big issue, but maybe I’d need an example of what you mean.

Like in IEP meetings, or those admin classes I have to sit through, I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing under the table the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen over and over.\n\nSometimes I’m shifting around in the chair so much that people glance over, and then I’m like, oh—okay, I’m doing it again. It’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive. Sitting still just feels… I don’t know, uncomfortable after a while. And those meetings can go on forever, with paperwork and goals and accommodations—sorry, I’m getting off track—but yes, the fidgeting happens a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in line or in conversations?\n\nPatient: Waiting my turn… I don’t know. In a line, I mean, I don’t love lines, but who does? If I’m at the grocery store and someone’s using coupons or there’s a price check, I’ll notice myself getting kind of impatient, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or just being a person with frozen food in the cart.\n\nIn conversations… maybe sometimes I jump in if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure that’s the same as “trouble waiting.” With teachers we’re always sort of overlapping—somebody mentions a student, then somebody else remembers a meeting, then the bell rings, and it’s all a little chaotic anyway.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not really sure. I haven’t thought of it as a big issue, but maybe I’d need an example of what you mean.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often have trouble organizing your tasks or activities?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Organizing tasks or activities… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, my work has a lot of moving parts—lesson plans, paperwork, meetings, emails, student schedules—and some of that is just the job, you know? Special ed is basically a stack of folders that somehow keeps growing.

I do use lists sometimes, but then I’ll have three different lists—one on a sticky note, one in my planner, one in my head—and then the sticky note ends up in a folder with something totally unrelated. Actually, yesterday I found a note from last month tucked into a packet about bus schedules, and I don’t even know why I put it there.

But I don’t know if that means I have “trouble organizing” in the way you mean. I usually get things done eventually. It can feel messy getting there, though.

up—refill water, check the copier, look in on my relative—even when I know it makes me look disrespectful.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or narrating what I’m doing, humming, or moving things around until someone asks me to please settle down.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that I feel like I’m constantly running on a motor lately, like I finish one thing at school or home and immediately jump into the next without ever being able to shut myself off.\n- key: S015\n  name: Excessive_Talking\n  description: Often talks excessively.\n  manifestation: I’ll go into a parent conference or a staff meeting meaning to be brief, and then I hear myself giving three examples, a story from my classroom, and ideas nobody asked for while the other person is clearly trying to wrap it up.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: I keep jumping into conversations between colleagues, parents, or my spouse and our relative because I think I’m helping, but I end up talking over them or taking charge before they’ve even had a chance to finish.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: My mother used to joke that even in elementary school I taught the class from my seat because I was always out of my chair, talking, or cutting in before the teacher finished directions. This was part of who I was long before adulthood, not something that just started with this family change.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work, the complaints have gotten serious enough that I was put on a corrective plan, and at home my spouse says caregiving routines are harder because people have to manage my energy on top of everything else. I feel like I’m damaging trust with colleagues and family even though I’m trying to help.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 8 months\n  manifestation: This level of trouble has been going on for about 8 months, since our older relative moved in with us.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 58 / special_education_teacher / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S014\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t always notice it at first, which is the weird part. Like in IEP meetings, or those admin classes I have to sit through, I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing under the table the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen over and over.\n\nSometimes I’m shifting around in the chair so much that people glance over, and then I’m like, oh—okay, I’m doing it again. It’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive. Sitting still just feels… I don’t know, uncomfortable after a while. And those meetings can go on forever, with paperwork and goals and accommodations—sorry, I’m getting off track—but yes, the fidgeting happens a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in line or in conversations?\n\nPatient: Waiting my turn… I don’t know. In a line, I mean, I don’t love lines, but who does? If I’m at the grocery store and someone’s using coupons or there’s a price check, I’ll notice myself getting kind of impatient, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or just being a person with frozen food in the cart.\n\nIn conversations… maybe sometimes I jump in if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure that’s the same as “trouble waiting.” With teachers we’re always sort of overlapping—somebody mentions a student, then somebody else remembers a meeting, then the bell rings, and it’s all a little chaotic anyway.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not really sure. I haven’t thought of it as a big issue, but maybe I’d need an example of what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Organizing tasks or activities… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, my work has a lot of moving parts—lesson plans, paperwork, meetings, emails, student schedules—and some of that is just the job, you know? Special ed is basically a stack of folders that somehow keeps growing.\n\nI do use lists sometimes, but then I’ll have three different lists—one on a sticky note, one in my planner, one in my head—and then the sticky note ends up in a folder with something totally unrelated. Actually, yesterday I found a note from last month tucked into a packet about bus schedules, and I don’t even know why I put it there.\n\nBut I don’t know if that means I have “trouble organizing” in the way you mean. I usually get things done eventually. It can feel messy getting there, though.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often dominate conversations with friends or family members outside of work?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Outside of work? Yeah… I mean, probably. My wife would say yes faster than I would.

I’ll start answering a simple question, like about dinner plans or something, and then I’m explaining the whole background of why I think the new grocery store layout is strange—actually they moved the bread aisle recently and it makes no sense—but, yeah, I can kind of take over without meaning to.

It happens at work too, maybe that’s where I notice it more. I’ll go into a parent conference or a staff meeting meaning to be brief, and then suddenly I hear myself giving three examples, a story from my classroom, and suggestions nobody asked for, and the other person is clearly trying to wrap it up. Then I’m like, okay, I should stop talking now. But I don’t always catch it in time.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.1, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I just feel like I can’t shut off. Like I’m constantly running on a motor lately. I’ll finish something at school, or at home, and before I even take a breath I’m onto the next thing.\n\nI’m a special education teacher, so there’s always something to do anyway, you know—papers, kids needing help, emails, and then I get home and it’s the same kind of thing, just different tasks. But it feels like it’s more than just being busy. It’s like my body and brain won’t let me stop moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. I don’t always realize it in the moment, but I’ll go into, say, a parent conference or a staff meeting thinking, “Okay, just keep it short,” and then suddenly I’m giving three examples, then I’m telling some story from my classroom, then I’m offering ideas nobody even asked for.\n\nAnd I can kind of see the other person doing that thing where they’re ready to wrap up—looking at the clock or shifting papers—but I’m still talking. It’s embarrassing afterward, because I really didn’t mean to take over the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do… more than I’d like to admit. I’ll hear two colleagues talking in the hallway, or a parent explaining something, and I’ll jump in because in my head I’m thinking, “Oh, I know how to help with this,” or “Let me clarify that.” But then I realize I’ve talked over them or kind of taken charge before they even finished their sentence.\n\nIt happens at home too—my spouse might be talking with a relative, and I’ll butt in like I’m solving the problem, when nobody asked me to. I don’t mean it as rude, but it comes out that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself acting on impulse, like doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Um… acting on impulse. I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I make quick decisions sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it “without thinking.” \n\nLike, in teaching you’re constantly reacting—one kid needs help, another situation pops up, the schedule changes—so you sort of have to move fast. And then I start thinking about how schools expect you to be flexible but also have everything planned, which is its own whole thing…\n\nSorry, I’m getting off track. I guess I’d need an example of what you mean by impulse. I’m not sure I’ve noticed that as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your personal life, outside of work or teaching?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not sure. I mean, probably everybody does that sometimes, right? Like buying something without really comparing prices, or saying yes to a dinner before checking the calendar—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by impulsive.\n\nAt home I tend to… well, actually, my wife would probably have a better answer to this than I do. She notices things before I do. Like I’ll start a house project because something is bothering me, and then suddenly the garage is a mess, and I’m looking for a tool I know I had in 1998—sorry, that’s not really answering.\n\nI guess I don’t clearly think of myself as “acting on impulse” in my personal life. I’d need you to define it a little more, because I’m not sure what counts.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems in your personal relationships due to saying or doing something without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like I’m trying to be hurtful, but I’ll jump into conversations—like my wife talking with a relative, or at work with colleagues or parents—and in my head I’m thinking, “Oh, I can help,” or “I know what they mean,” and then I’m already talking before they’re done.\n\nAnd then it turns into me taking over the conversation, which… yeah, people don’t always appreciate that. My wife will give me that look, like, “Let them finish,” and I realize I kind of barged in. It causes tension because I think I’m helping, but it comes across like I’m not listening or I’m trying to run the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks or projects you've started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one. I mean… tasks and projects, that could mean a lot of things. At work there are always things that are “started” but not really finished because somebody changes the plan, or the meeting gets moved, or a student needs something right then.\n\nAt home, I guess I have projects that sit around, but I don’t know if that’s unusual. Like, there’s always something half-done in a house, right? You fix one thing and then notice the drawer sticks, and then you’re in the garage looking for screws, and then—well, anyway.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed it as a major problem, but I might need a more specific example of what you mean by “trouble completing.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Easily distracted by things around me… I’m not sure. I mean, it depends where I am, I guess. If there’s a lot going on, like noise in the hallway or someone moving around, I notice it, but I don’t know if that’s more than other people.\n\nAt school there’s always something happening—kids coming in and out, announcements, papers, somebody needing a pen—and so it’s hard to separate “I’m distracted” from “the room is just busy.” At home, too, my wife will say something while I’m looking for my glasses, and then I’ll notice the mail on the counter, and then—sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I don’t have a clear answer. I notice things around me, but I don’t know if I’d call it a big problem unless you mean something specific.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t always notice it at first, which is the weird part. Like in IEP meetings, or those admin classes I have to sit through, I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing under the table the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen over and over.\n\nSometimes I’m shifting around in the chair so much that people glance over, and then I’m like, oh—okay, I’m doing it again. In a line, I mean, I don’t love lines, but who does? I haven’t thought of it as a big issue, but maybe I’d need an example of what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Organizing tasks or activities… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, my work has a lot of moving parts—lesson plans, paperwork, meetings, emails, student schedules—and some of that is just the job, you know? Special ed is basically a stack of folders that somehow keeps growing.\n\nI do use lists sometimes, but then I’ll have three different lists—one on a sticky note, one in my planner, one in my head—and then the sticky note ends up in a folder with something totally unrelated. Yeah… yes, I do that. I don’t always notice it until I’m already halfway into my answer.

Like with my wife, if she’s talking to one of our relatives and I think I know what she’s trying to explain, I’ll jump in and start “helping,” but really I’m talking over her. But it feels like it’s more than just being busy. It’s like my body and brain won’t let me stop moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. I don’t always realize it in the moment, but I’ll go into, say, a parent conference or a staff meeting thinking, “Okay, just keep it short,” and then suddenly I’m giving three examples, then I’m telling some story from my classroom, then I’m offering ideas nobody even asked for.\n\nAnd I can kind of see the other person doing that thing where they’re ready to wrap up—looking at the clock or shifting papers—but I’m still talking. It’s embarrassing afterward, because I really didn’t mean to take over the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do… more than I’d like to admit. I’ll hear two colleagues talking in the hallway, or a parent explaining something, and I’ll jump in because in my head I’m thinking, “Oh, I know how to help with this,” or “Let me clarify that.” But then I realize I’ve talked over them or kind of taken charge before they even finished their sentence.\n\nIt happens at home too—my spouse might be talking with a relative, and I’ll butt in like I’m solving the problem, when nobody asked me to. I don’t mean it as rude, but it comes out that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself acting on impulse, like doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Um… acting on impulse. I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I make quick decisions sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it “without thinking.” \n\nLike, in teaching you’re constantly reacting—one kid needs help, another situation pops up, the schedule changes—so you sort of have to move fast. And then I start thinking about how schools expect you to be flexible but also have everything planned, which is its own whole thing…\n\nSorry, I’m getting off track. I guess I’d need an example of what you mean by impulse. I’m not sure I’ve noticed that as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your personal life, outside of work or teaching?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not sure. I mean, probably everybody does that sometimes, right? Like buying something without really comparing prices, or saying yes to a dinner before checking the calendar—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by impulsive.\n\nAt home I tend to… well, actually, my wife would probably have a better answer to this than I do. She notices things before I do. Like I’ll start a house project because something is bothering me, and then suddenly the garage is a mess, and I’m looking for a tool I know I had in 1998—sorry, that’s not really answering.\n\nI guess I don’t clearly think of myself as “acting on impulse” in my personal life. I’d need you to define it a little more, because I’m not sure what counts.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems in your personal relationships due to saying or doing something without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like I’m trying to be hurtful, but I’ll jump into conversations—like my wife talking with a relative, or at work with colleagues or parents—and in my head I’m thinking, “Oh, I can help,” or “I know what they mean,” and then I’m already talking before they’re done.\n\nAnd then it turns into me taking over the conversation, which… yeah, people don’t always appreciate that. My wife will give me that look, like, “Let them finish,” and I realize I kind of barged in. It causes tension because I think I’m helping, but it comes across like I’m not listening or I’m trying to run the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks or projects you've started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one. I mean… tasks and projects, that could mean a lot of things. At work there are always things that are “started” but not really finished because somebody changes the plan, or the meeting gets moved, or a student needs something right then.\n\nAt home, I guess I have projects that sit around, but I don’t know if that’s unusual. Like, there’s always something half-done in a house, right? You fix one thing and then notice the drawer sticks, and then you’re in the garage looking for screws, and then—well, anyway.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed it as a major problem, but I might need a more specific example of what you mean by “trouble completing.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Easily distracted by things around me… I’m not sure. I mean, it depends where I am, I guess. If there’s a lot going on, like noise in the hallway or someone moving around, I notice it, but I don’t know if that’s more than other people.\n\nAt school there’s always something happening—kids coming in and out, announcements, papers, somebody needing a pen—and so it’s hard to separate “I’m distracted” from “the room is just busy.” At home, too, my wife will say something while I’m looking for my glasses, and then I’ll notice the mail on the counter, and then—sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I don’t have a clear answer. I notice things around me, but I don’t know if I’d call it a big problem unless you mean something specific.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t always notice it at first, which is the weird part. Like in IEP meetings, or those admin classes I have to sit through, I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing under the table the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen over and over.\n\nSometimes I’m shifting around in the chair so much that people glance over, and then I’m like, oh—okay, I’m doing it again. It’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive. Sitting still just feels… I don’t know, uncomfortable after a while. And those meetings can go on forever, with paperwork and goals and accommodations—sorry, I’m getting off track—but yes, the fidgeting happens a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in line or in conversations?\n\nPatient: Waiting my turn… I don’t know. In a line, I mean, I don’t love lines, but who does? If I’m at the grocery store and someone’s using coupons or there’s a price check, I’ll notice myself getting kind of impatient, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or just being a person with frozen food in the cart.\n\nIn conversations… maybe sometimes I jump in if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure that’s the same as “trouble waiting.” With teachers we’re always sort of overlapping—somebody mentions a student, then somebody else remembers a meeting, then the bell rings, and it’s all a little chaotic anyway.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not really sure. I haven’t thought of it as a big issue, but maybe I’d need an example of what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Organizing tasks or activities… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, my work has a lot of moving parts—lesson plans, paperwork, meetings, emails, student schedules—and some of that is just the job, you know? Special ed is basically a stack of folders that somehow keeps growing.\n\nI do use lists sometimes, but then I’ll have three different lists—one on a sticky note, one in my planner, one in my head—and then the sticky note ends up in a folder with something totally unrelated. Actually, yesterday I found a note from last month tucked into a packet about bus schedules, and I don’t even know why I put it there.\n\nBut I don’t know if that means I have “trouble organizing” in the way you mean. I usually get things done eventually. It can feel messy getting there, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you often dominate conversations with friends or family members outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? Yeah… I mean, probably. My wife would say yes faster than I would.\n\nI’ll start answering a simple question, like about dinner plans or something, and then I’m explaining the whole background of why I think the new grocery store layout is strange—actually they moved the bread aisle recently and it makes no sense—but, yeah, I can kind of take over without meaning to.\n\nIt happens at work too, maybe that’s where I notice it more. I’ll go into a parent conference or a staff meeting meaning to be brief, and then suddenly I hear myself giving three examples, a story from my classroom, and suggestions nobody asked for, and the other person is clearly trying to wrap it up. Then I’m like, okay, I should stop talking now. But I don’t always catch it in time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before questions have been completed in conversations with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do that. I don’t always notice it until I’m already halfway into my answer.\n\nLike with my wife, if she’s talking to one of our relatives and I think I know what she’s trying to explain, I’ll jump in and start “helping,” but really I’m talking over her. Or with parents at school, they’ll start asking something and before they even finish the question, I’m already answering or steering the conversation because I think I know where it’s going.\n\nAnd then sometimes I realize, oh, they weren’t done. I kind of took charge there. I don’t mean it in a rude way, but it happens a lot.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh yeah, that goes way back. My mother used to joke that I “taught the class from my seat” in elementary school, because I was always popping out of my chair or talking when I wasn’t supposed to.

And I’d cut in before the teacher even finished giving directions—like I already knew what she was going to say, even when I probably didn’t. I think about that now with my own students sometimes, actually, how teachers must have handled kids like me back then… but yes, it wasn’t something that just showed up later in life. That was kind of me from early on.

If there’s a lot going on, like noise in the hallway or someone moving around, I notice it, but I don’t know if that’s more than other people.\n\nAt school there’s always something happening—kids coming in and out, announcements, papers, somebody needing a pen—and so it’s hard to separate “I’m distracted” from “the room is just busy.” At home, too, my wife will say something while I’m looking for my glasses, and then I’ll notice the mail on the counter, and then—sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I don’t have a clear answer. I notice things around me, but I don’t know if I’d call it a big problem unless you mean something specific.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t always notice it at first, which is the weird part. Like in IEP meetings, or those admin classes I have to sit through, I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing under the table the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen over and over.\n\nSometimes I’m shifting around in the chair so much that people glance over, and then I’m like, oh—okay, I’m doing it again. It’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive. Sitting still just feels… I don’t know, uncomfortable after a while. And those meetings can go on forever, with paperwork and goals and accommodations—sorry, I’m getting off track—but yes, the fidgeting happens a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in line or in conversations?\n\nPatient: Waiting my turn… I don’t know. In a line, I mean, I don’t love lines, but who does? If I’m at the grocery store and someone’s using coupons or there’s a price check, I’ll notice myself getting kind of impatient, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or just being a person with frozen food in the cart.\n\nIn conversations… maybe sometimes I jump in if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure that’s the same as “trouble waiting.” With teachers we’re always sort of overlapping—somebody mentions a student, then somebody else remembers a meeting, then the bell rings, and it’s all a little chaotic anyway.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not really sure. I haven’t thought of it as a big issue, but maybe I’d need an example of what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Organizing tasks or activities… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, my work has a lot of moving parts—lesson plans, paperwork, meetings, emails, student schedules—and some of that is just the job, you know? Special ed is basically a stack of folders that somehow keeps growing.\n\nI do use lists sometimes, but then I’ll have three different lists—one on a sticky note, one in my planner, one in my head—and then the sticky note ends up in a folder with something totally unrelated. Actually, yesterday I found a note from last month tucked into a packet about bus schedules, and I don’t even know why I put it there.\n\nBut I don’t know if that means I have “trouble organizing” in the way you mean. I usually get things done eventually. It can feel messy getting there, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you often dominate conversations with friends or family members outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? Yeah… I mean, probably. My wife would say yes faster than I would.\n\nI’ll start answering a simple question, like about dinner plans or something, and then I’m explaining the whole background of why I think the new grocery store layout is strange—actually they moved the bread aisle recently and it makes no sense—but, yeah, I can kind of take over without meaning to.\n\nIt happens at work too, maybe that’s where I notice it more. I’ll go into a parent conference or a staff meeting meaning to be brief, and then suddenly I hear myself giving three examples, a story from my classroom, and suggestions nobody asked for, and the other person is clearly trying to wrap it up. Then I’m like, okay, I should stop talking now. But I don’t always catch it in time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before questions have been completed in conversations with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do that. I don’t always notice it until I’m already halfway into my answer.\n\nLike with my wife, if she’s talking to one of our relatives and I think I know what she’s trying to explain, I’ll jump in and start “helping,” but really I’m talking over her. Or with parents at school, they’ll start asking something and before they even finish the question, I’m already answering or steering the conversation because I think I know where it’s going.\n\nAnd then sometimes I realize, oh, they weren’t done. I kind of took charge there. I don’t mean it in a rude way, but it happens a lot.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with impulsivity or behavior when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Oh yeah, that goes way back. My mother used to joke that I “taught the class from my seat” in elementary school, because I was always popping out of my chair or talking when I wasn’t supposed to.\n\nAnd I’d cut in before the teacher even finished giving directions—like I already knew what she was going to say, even when I probably didn’t. I think about that now with my own students sometimes, actually, how teachers must have handled kids like me back then… but yes, it wasn’t something that just showed up later in life. That was kind of me from early on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. The main thing that comes to mind is conversations. I’ll know I should wait—like I can literally be thinking, “Don’t jump in, let them finish”—and then I’m already talking.

It happens with colleagues, with parents at school, and at home too. My wife might be talking to a relative, and I think I’m helping by clarifying something or moving the conversation along, but really I’ve taken over before either of them has finished. Same with a parent meeting—I hear the first half of a question and I’m off answering what I think they’re asking.

And then afterward I feel bad, because I can see that I talked over them or kind of grabbed the steering wheel. I know it can cause problems, but in the moment it’s like my mouth gets there before the rest of me does.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I just feel like I can’t shut off. Like I’m constantly running on a motor lately. But it feels like it’s more than just being busy. It’s like my body and brain won’t let me stop moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. I don’t always realize it in the moment, but I’ll go into, say, a parent conference or a staff meeting thinking, “Okay, just keep it short,” and then suddenly I’m giving three examples, then I’m telling some story from my classroom, then I’m offering ideas nobody even asked for.\n\nAnd I can kind of see the other person doing that thing where they’re ready to wrap up—looking at the clock or shifting papers—but I’m still talking. I guess I’d need an example of what you mean by impulse. I’m not sure I’ve noticed that as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your personal life, outside of work or teaching?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not sure. I mean, probably everybody does that sometimes, right? Like buying something without really comparing prices, or saying yes to a dinner before checking the calendar—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by impulsive.\n\nAt home I tend to… well, actually, my wife would probably have a better answer to this than I do. She notices things before I do. Like I’ll start a house project because something is bothering me, and then suddenly the garage is a mess, and I’m looking for a tool I know I had in 1998—sorry, that’s not really answering.\n\nI guess I don’t clearly think of myself as “acting on impulse” in my personal life. I’d need you to define it a little more, because I’m not sure what counts.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems in your personal relationships due to saying or doing something without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like I’m trying to be hurtful, but I’ll jump into conversations—like my wife talking with a relative, or at work with colleagues or parents—and in my head I’m thinking, “Oh, I can help,” or “I know what they mean,” and then I’m already talking before they’re done.\n\nAnd then it turns into me taking over the conversation, which… yeah, people don’t always appreciate that. My wife will give me that look, like, “Let them finish,” and I realize I kind of barged in. It causes tension because I think I’m helping, but it comes across like I’m not listening or I’m trying to run the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks or projects you've started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one. I mean… tasks and projects, that could mean a lot of things. At work there are always things that are “started” but not really finished because somebody changes the plan, or the meeting gets moved, or a student needs something right then.\n\nAt home, I guess I have projects that sit around, but I don’t know if that’s unusual. Like, there’s always something half-done in a house, right? You fix one thing and then notice the drawer sticks, and then you’re in the garage looking for screws, and then—well, anyway.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed it as a major problem, but I might need a more specific example of what you mean by “trouble completing.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Easily distracted by things around me… I’m not sure. I mean, it depends where I am, I guess. If there’s a lot going on, like noise in the hallway or someone moving around, I notice it, but I don’t know if that’s more than other people.\n\nAt school there’s always something happening—kids coming in and out, announcements, papers, somebody needing a pen—and so it’s hard to separate “I’m distracted” from “the room is just busy.” At home, too, my wife will say something while I’m looking for my glasses, and then I’ll notice the mail on the counter, and then—sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I don’t have a clear answer. I notice things around me, but I don’t know if I’d call it a big problem unless you mean something specific.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t always notice it at first, which is the weird part. Like in IEP meetings, or those admin classes I have to sit through, I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing under the table the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen over and over.\n\nSometimes I’m shifting around in the chair so much that people glance over, and then I’m like, oh—okay, I’m doing it again. It’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive. Sitting still just feels… I don’t know, uncomfortable after a while. And those meetings can go on forever, with paperwork and goals and accommodations—sorry, I’m getting off track—but yes, the fidgeting happens a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in line or in conversations?\n\nPatient: Waiting my turn… I don’t know. In a line, I mean, I don’t love lines, but who does? If I’m at the grocery store and someone’s using coupons or there’s a price check, I’ll notice myself getting kind of impatient, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or just being a person with frozen food in the cart.\n\nIn conversations… maybe sometimes I jump in if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure that’s the same as “trouble waiting.” With teachers we’re always sort of overlapping—somebody mentions a student, then somebody else remembers a meeting, then the bell rings, and it’s all a little chaotic anyway.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not really sure. I haven’t thought of it as a big issue, but maybe I’d need an example of what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Organizing tasks or activities… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, my work has a lot of moving parts—lesson plans, paperwork, meetings, emails, student schedules—and some of that is just the job, you know? Special ed is basically a stack of folders that somehow keeps growing.\n\nI do use lists sometimes, but then I’ll have three different lists—one on a sticky note, one in my planner, one in my head—and then the sticky note ends up in a folder with something totally unrelated. Actually, yesterday I found a note from last month tucked into a packet about bus schedules, and I don’t even know why I put it there.\n\nBut I don’t know if that means I have “trouble organizing” in the way you mean. I usually get things done eventually. It can feel messy getting there, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you often dominate conversations with friends or family members outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? Yeah… I mean, probably.   {

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Predominantly Hyperactive/Impulsive Presentation). It’s like my body and brain won’t let me stop moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. I don’t always realize it in the moment, but I’ll go into, say, a parent conference or a staff meeting thinking, “Okay, just keep it short,” and then suddenly I’m giving three examples, then I’m telling some story from my classroom, then I’m offering ideas nobody even asked for.\n\nAnd I can kind of see the other person doing that thing where they’re ready to wrap up—looking at the clock or shifting papers—but I’m still talking. I’ll hear two colleagues talking in the hallway, or a parent explaining something, and I’ll jump in because in my head I’m thinking, “Oh, I know how to help with this,” or “Let me clarify that.” But then I realize I’ve talked over them or kind of taken charge before they even finished their sentence.\n\nIt happens at home too—my spouse might be talking with a relative, and I’ll butt in like I’m solving the problem, when nobody asked me to. I guess I’d need an example of what you mean by impulse. I’m not sure I’ve noticed that as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your personal life, outside of work or teaching?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not sure. Like buying something without really comparing prices, or saying yes to a dinner before checking the calendar—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by impulsive.\n\nAt home I tend to… well, actually, my wife would probably have a better answer to this than I do. Not like I’m trying to be hurtful, but I’ll jump into conversations—like my wife talking with a relative, or at work with colleagues or parents—and in my head I’m thinking, “Oh, I can help,” or “I know what they mean,” and then I’m already talking before they’re done.\n\nAnd then it turns into me taking over the conversation, which… yeah, people don’t always appreciate that. I mean… tasks and projects, that could mean a lot of things. Like, there’s always something half-done in a house, right? You fix one thing and then notice the drawer sticks, and then you’re in the garage looking for screws, and then—well, anyway.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed it as a major problem, but I might need a more specific example of what you mean by “trouble completing.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Easily distracted by things around me… I’m not sure. I mean, it depends where I am, I guess. If there’s a lot going on, like noise in the hallway or someone moving around, I notice it, but I don’t know if that’s more than other people.\n\nAt school there’s always something happening—kids coming in and out, announcements, papers, somebody needing a pen—and so it’s hard to separate “I’m distracted” from “the room is just busy.” At home, too, my wife will say something while I’m looking for my glasses, and then I’ll notice the mail on the counter, and then—sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I don’t have a clear answer. I notice things around me, but I don’t know if I’d call it a big problem unless you mean something specific.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t always notice it at first, which is the weird part. Like in IEP meetings, or those admin classes I have to sit through, I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing under the table the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen over and over.\n\nSometimes I’m shifting around in the chair so much that people glance over, and then I’m like, oh—okay, I’m doing it again. It’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive. Sitting still just feels… I don’t know, uncomfortable after a while. And those meetings can go on forever, with paperwork and goals and accommodations—sorry, I’m getting off track—but yes, the fidgeting happens a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in line or in conversations?\n\nPatient: Waiting my turn… I don’t know. In a line, I mean, I don’t love lines, but who does? If I’m at the grocery store and someone’s using coupons or there’s a price check, I’ll notice myself getting kind of impatient, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or just being a person with frozen food in the cart.\n\nIn conversations… maybe sometimes I jump in if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure that’s the same as “trouble waiting.” With teachers we’re always sort of overlapping—somebody mentions a student, then somebody else remembers a meeting, then the bell rings, and it’s all a little chaotic anyway.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not really sure. I haven’t thought of it as a big issue, but maybe I’d need an example of what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Organizing tasks or activities… I’m not sure how to answer that. I usually get things done eventually. My wife would say yes faster than I would.\n\nI’ll start answering a simple question, like about dinner plans or something, and then I’m explaining the whole background of why I think the new grocery store layout is strange—actually they moved the bread aisle recently and it makes no sense—but, yeah, I can kind of take over without meaning to.\n\nIt happens at work too, maybe that’s where I notice it more. Not like I’m trying to be hurtful, but I’ll jump into conversations—like my wife talking with a relative, or at work with colleagues or parents—and in my head I’m thinking, “Oh, I can help,” or “I know what they mean,” and then I’m already talking before they’re done.\n\nAnd then it turns into me taking over the conversation, which… yeah, people don’t always appreciate that. My wife will give me that look, like, “Let them finish,” and I realize I kind of barged in. It causes tension because I think I’m helping, but it comes across like I’m not listening or I’m trying to run the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks or projects you've started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one. I mean… tasks and projects, that could mean a lot of things. At work there are always things that are “started” but not really finished because somebody changes the plan, or the meeting gets moved, or a student needs something right then.\n\nAt home, I guess I have projects that sit around, but I don’t know if that’s unusual. Like, there’s always something half-done in a house, right? You fix one thing and then notice the drawer sticks, and then you’re in the garage looking for screws, and then—well, anyway.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed it as a major problem, but I might need a more specific example of what you mean by “trouble completing.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Easily distracted by things around me… I’m not sure. I mean, it depends where I am, I guess. If there’s a lot going on, like noise in the hallway or someone moving around, I notice it, but I don’t know if that’s more than other people.\n\nAt school there’s always something happening—kids coming in and out, announcements, papers, somebody needing a pen—and so it’s hard to separate “I’m distracted” from “the room is just busy.” At home, too, my wife will say something while I’m looking for my glasses, and then I’ll notice the mail on the counter, and then—sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I don’t have a clear answer. I notice things around me, but I don’t know if I’d call it a big problem unless you mean something specific.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I don’t always notice it at first, which is the weird part. Like in IEP meetings, or those admin classes I have to sit through, I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing under the table the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen over and over.\n\nSometimes I’m shifting around in the chair so much that people glance over, and then I’m like, oh—okay, I’m doing it again. It’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive. Sitting still just feels… I don’t know, uncomfortable after a while. And those meetings can go on forever, with paperwork and goals and accommodations—sorry, I’m getting off track—but yes, the fidgeting happens a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in line or in conversations?\n\nPatient: Waiting my turn… I don’t know. In a line, I mean, I don’t love lines, but who does? If I’m at the grocery store and someone’s using coupons or there’s a price check, I’ll notice myself getting kind of impatient, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or just being a person with frozen food in the cart.\n\nIn conversations… maybe sometimes I jump in if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure that’s the same as “trouble waiting.” With teachers we’re always sort of overlapping—somebody mentions a student, then somebody else remembers a meeting, then the bell rings, and it’s all a little chaotic anyway.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not really sure. I haven’t thought of it as a big issue, but maybe I’d need an example of what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Organizing tasks or activities… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, my work has a lot of moving parts—lesson plans, paperwork, meetings, emails, student schedules—and some of that is just the job, you know? Special ed is basically a stack of folders that somehow keeps growing.\n\nI do use lists sometimes, but then I’ll have three different lists—one on a sticky note, one in my planner, one in my head—and then the sticky note ends up in a folder with something totally unrelated. Actually, yesterday I found a note from last month tucked into a packet about bus schedules, and I don’t even know why I put it there.\n\nBut I don’t know if that means I have “trouble organizing” in the way you mean. I usually get things done eventually. It can feel messy getting there, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you often dominate conversations with friends or family members outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? Yeah… I mean, probably. My wife would say yes faster than I would.\n\nI’ll start answering a simple question, like about dinner plans or something, and then I’m explaining the whole background of why I think the new grocery store layout is strange—actually they moved the bread aisle recently and it makes no sense—but, yeah, I can kind of take over without meaning to.\n\nIt happens at work too, maybe that’s where I notice it more. I’ll go into a parent conference or a staff meeting meaning to be brief, and then suddenly I hear myself giving three examples, a story from my classroom, and suggestions nobody asked for, and the other person is clearly trying to wrap it up. Then I’m like, okay, I should stop talking now. But I don’t always catch it in time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before questions have been completed in conversations with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do that. I don’t always notice it until I’m already halfway into my answer.\n\nLike with my wife, if she’s talking to one of our relatives and I think I know what she’s trying to explain, I’ll jump in and start “helping,” but really I’m talking over her. Or with parents at school, they’ll start asking something and before they even finish the question, I’m already answering or steering the conversation because I think I know where it’s going.\n\nAnd then sometimes I realize, oh, they weren’t done. I kind of took charge there. I don’t mean it in a rude way, but it happens a lot.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with impulsivity or behavior when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Oh yeah, that goes way back. My mother used to joke that I “taught the class from my seat” in elementary school, because I was always popping out of my chair or talking when I wasn’t supposed to.\n\nAnd I’d cut in before the teacher even finished giving directions—like I already knew what she was going to say, even when I probably didn’t. I think about that now with my own students sometimes, actually, how teachers must have handled kids like me back then… but yes, it wasn’t something that just showed up later in life. That was kind of me from early on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been in a situation where you felt like you couldn't control your impulses, even when you knew it might cause problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. The main thing that comes to mind is conversations. I’ll know I should wait—like I can literally be thinking, “Don’t jump in, let them finish”—and then I’m already talking.\n\nIt happens with colleagues, with parents at school, and at home too. My wife might be talking to a relative, and I think I’m helping by clarifying something or moving the conversation along, but really I’ve taken over before either of them has finished. Same with a parent meeting—I hear the first half of a question and I’m off answering what I think they’re asking.\n\nAnd then afterward I feel bad, because I can see that I talked over them or kind of grabbed the steering wheel. I know it can cause problems, but in the moment it’s like my mouth gets there before the rest of me does.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining focus during tasks or activities that you're not particularly interested in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… if I’m interested in something, I can stay with it pretty well, sometimes too long, honestly. But if it’s something I don’t care about, I don’t know—I'd have to think about examples.\n\nLike paperwork at school can be rough, but everybody hates paperwork, right? Special ed paperwork is its own whole universe, and half the time the forms change or somebody wants it worded a certain way… sorry, I’m getting off track.\n\nI guess I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s a focus issue exactly, or just that some tasks are boring and I put them off. So I’m not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. And those meetings can go on forever, with paperwork and goals and accommodations—sorry, I’m getting off track—but yes, the fidgeting happens a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in line or in conversations?\n\nPatient: Waiting my turn… I don’t know. In a line, I mean, I don’t love lines, but who does? If I’m at the grocery store and someone’s using coupons or there’s a price check, I’ll notice myself getting kind of impatient, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or just being a person with frozen food in the cart.\n\nIn conversations… maybe sometimes I jump in if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure that’s the same as “trouble waiting.” With teachers we’re always sort of overlapping—somebody mentions a student, then somebody else remembers a meeting, then the bell rings, and it’s all a little chaotic anyway.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not really sure. I haven’t thought of it as a big issue, but maybe I’d need an example of what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Organizing tasks or activities… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, my work has a lot of moving parts—lesson plans, paperwork, meetings, emails, student schedules—and some of that is just the job, you know? Special ed is basically a stack of folders that somehow keeps growing.\n\nI do use lists sometimes, but then I’ll have three different lists—one on a sticky note, one in my planner, one in my head—and then the sticky note ends up in a folder with something totally unrelated. Actually, yesterday I found a note from last month tucked into a packet about bus schedules, and I don’t even know why I put it there.\n\nBut I don’t know if that means I have “trouble organizing” in the way you mean. I usually get things done eventually. It can feel messy getting there, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you often dominate conversations with friends or family members outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? Yeah… I mean, probably. My wife would say yes faster than I would.\n\nI’ll start answering a simple question, like about dinner plans or something, and then I’m explaining the whole background of why I think the new grocery store layout is strange—actually they moved the bread aisle recently and it makes no sense—but, yeah, I can kind of take over without meaning to.\n\nIt happens at work too, maybe that’s where I notice it more. I’ll go into a parent conference or a staff meeting meaning to be brief, and then suddenly I hear myself giving three examples, a story from my classroom, and suggestions nobody asked for, and the other person is clearly trying to wrap it up. Then I’m like, okay, I should stop talking now. But I don’t always catch it in time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before questions have been completed in conversations with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do that. I don’t always notice it until I’m already halfway into my answer.\n\nLike with my wife, if she’s talking to one of our relatives and I think I know what she’s trying to explain, I’ll jump in and start “helping,” but really I’m talking over her. Or with parents at school, they’ll start asking something and before they even finish the question, I’m already answering or steering the conversation because I think I know where it’s going.\n\nAnd then sometimes I realize, oh, they weren’t done. I kind of took charge there. I don’t mean it in a rude way, but it happens a lot.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with impulsivity or behavior when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Oh yeah, that goes way back. My mother used to joke that I “taught the class from my seat” in elementary school, because I was always popping out of my chair or talking when I wasn’t supposed to.\n\nAnd I’d cut in before the teacher even finished giving directions—like I already knew what she was going to say, even when I probably didn’t. I think about that now with my own students sometimes, actually, how teachers must have handled kids like me back then… but yes, it wasn’t something that just showed up later in life. That was kind of me from early on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been in a situation where you felt like you couldn't control your impulses, even when you knew it might cause problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. The main thing that comes to mind is conversations. I’ll know I should wait—like I can literally be thinking, “Don’t jump in, let them finish”—and then I’m already talking.\n\nIt happens with colleagues, with parents at school, and at home too. My wife might be talking to a relative, and I think I’m helping by clarifying something or moving the conversation along, but really I’ve taken over before either of them has finished. Same with a parent meeting—I hear the first half of a question and I’m off answering what I think they’re asking.\n\nAnd then afterward I feel bad, because I can see that I talked over them or kind of grabbed the steering wheel. I know it can cause problems, but in the moment it’s like my mouth gets there before the rest of me does.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining focus during tasks or activities that you're not particularly interested in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… if I’m interested in something, I can stay with it pretty well, sometimes too long, honestly. But if it’s something I don’t care about, I don’t know—I'd have to think about examples.\n\nLike paperwork at school can be rough, but everybody hates paperwork, right? Special ed paperwork is its own whole universe, and half the time the forms change or somebody wants it worded a certain way… sorry, I’m getting off track.\n\nI guess I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s a focus issue exactly, or just that some tasks are boring and I put them off. So I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these kinds of symptoms, like not being able to slow down or interrupting others, consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Well, not consistently throughout all of adulthood, no—not at this level. I mean, I’ve always been kind of a fast talker, and my wife would probably have opinions about that, but the real trouble with it feeling like I can’t slow myself down has been more recent.\n\nI’d say it’s been about eight months now, since our older relative moved in with us. That kind of changed the whole rhythm of the house—mealtimes, conversations, who’s needing what—and I think that’s when it started feeling much harder to manage.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

But if it’s something I don’t care about, I don’t know—I'd have to think about examples.\n\nLike paperwork at school can be rough, but everybody hates paperwork, right? Special ed paperwork is its own whole universe, and half the time the forms change or somebody wants it worded a certain way… sorry, I’m getting off track.\n\nI guess I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s a focus issue exactly, or just that some tasks are boring and I put them off. So I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these kinds of symptoms, like not being able to slow down or interrupting others, consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Well, not consistently throughout all of adulthood, no—not at this level. I mean, I’ve always been kind of a fast talker, and my wife would probably have opinions about that, but the real trouble with it feeling like I can’t slow myself down has been more recent.\n\nI’d say it’s been about eight months now, since our older relative moved in with us. That kind of changed the whole rhythm of the house—mealtimes, conversations, who’s needing what—and I think that’s when it started feeling much harder to manage.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these symptoms, like interrupting others or not being able to slow down, caused any significant problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 58 / special_education_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"At work, the complaints have gotten serious enough that I was put on a corrective plan, and at home my spouse says caregiving routines are harder because people have to manage my energy on top of everything else. I feel like I’m damaging trust with colleagues and family even though I’m trying to help.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… unfortunately, yes. At work it’s gotten pretty serious. I mean, schools are stressful places anyway—there’s always some new form, some new meeting, someone needing something five minutes ago—but this has gone past just “he talks too much.”

I’ve had enough complaints that they put me on a corrective plan. That was… humiliating, honestly. Because in my head I’m trying to help, trying to keep things moving, but what people experience is me cutting them off or taking over.

And at home it’s affecting things too. My wife has said the caregiving routines are harder because everybody’s already managing a lot, and then they also have to manage my energy on top of it. That one hurt to hear.

So yes, it’s causing problems. I feel like I’m damaging trust with colleagues and with my family, even though that’s the opposite of what I’m trying to do.
